# GIOVANNI PAOLO OLIVA
## OF THE SOCIETY OF JESUS
## SUPERIOR GENERAL.

<note>Giovanni Paolo Oliva (1600–1681) was the 11th Superior General of the Jesuits, known for his close relationship with the Roman aristocracy and his support of the arts and sciences.</note>

Since the work titled **China Illustrated**, written by **Father ATHANASIUS KIRCHER**, a Priest of our Society <note>The Society of Jesus, or the Jesuits</note>, has been reviewed by three theologians of the same Society, and they have approved that it may be published, we grant the authority for it to be committed to the press, if it so pleases those to whom it pertains: in testimony of which we provide these letters signed by our own hand and secured with our Seal. Given at Rome, November 14, 1664.

->GIOVANNI PAOLO OLIVA.<-

October 21, 2010

# TO THE VENERABLE
### *AND*
# MOST RELIGIOUS FATHER
# FATHER GIOVANNI PAOLO
# OLIVA,
### *Supreme Moderator of the Society* of JESUS. <note>Giovanni Paolo Oliva (1600–1681) was the 11th Superior General of the Jesuit Order.</note>

<image-desc>A large historiated woodcut initial 'S' decorated with intricate floral and foliate scrolls, featuring a central vase or urn motif.</image-desc>

**MOST RELIGIOUS FATHER**, there presents itself before your venerable gaze, in this new offspring of my intellect, that grand and almost immeasurable Empire of the Tartars and the Chinese <note>original: "Tartarorum Sinarumque Imperium"; referring to the Qing Dynasty, where the Manchu (Tartar) rulers governed China.</note>, administered by the absolute dominion and will of a single Monarch—a fact that may seem marvelous to anyone. It is indeed of such vastness that, within the circuit of the earthly globe, should you seek a power greater, larger, or more populous, you would find no equal to it. You may see the Kingdom of China alone so furnished with countless and most flourishing cities, that if you were to call any one of them an entire Province, you would hardly be speaking an absurdity. It is so cultivated with frequent—

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of the book to Giovanni Paolo Oliva, the Superior General of the Jesuits, introducing China and Tartary as a vast and peerless empire under a single ruler.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Paolo Oliva, Society of Jesus, Jesuits, China, Tartary, Qing Dynasty, Monarchy, Geography</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>The catchword "dum" indicates the text continues onto the next page with the word "while" or "until." This page focuses on the physical and spiritual state of the Chinese and Tartar empires.</meta>

## DEDICATION.

...both in Towns, Castles, Villages, Shrines, and Temples, so that if it were enclosed on all sides by that wall of three hundred leagues <note>The Great Wall of China; a league here refers to a traditional unit of distance, roughly 3 miles</note>—memorable throughout all ages—all of *China*, as vast as it is, might not undeservedly be called a single City rather than a Kingdom. Within it, you may see such an abundance of all things necessary for the human race, that what the wise industry of Nature has distributed sparingly among the other Kingdoms of the World, she seems to have gathered in its entirety within this single realm. I shall not speak in this place of the supreme vastness of the Tartar Kingdom <note>Referring to the regions of Manchuria and Mongolia</note>, which extends to such a great breadth that no one has yet been found who has discovered the borders of its regions stretching so far and wide. Since, therefore, the Empire which I have described in this Work is of such a kind and magnitude, this one thing is most deeply deplored by all who burn with zeal for promoting Divine glory: that in such an immensity of nations and peoples, countless populations of unknown parts are still to be found, over whom a thick mist of errors is spread; and being established in darkness and the shadow of death <note>original: "in tenebris & umbra mortis," a biblical reference to spiritual ignorance</note>, the way of truth is not yet [revealed] due to the scarcity of workers...

<summary>The author describes China as a singular, self-sufficient "city-kingdom" protected by its Great Wall and laments that despite its vastness and the neighboring Tartar lands, many inhabitants remain in spiritual darkness.</summary>
<keywords>China, Great Wall, Tartary, geography, missionary zeal, Divine glory, abundance of nature</keywords>

# D E D I C A T I O N

until it became known, the light of justice had not yet shone forth. For although now, through the course of ten lustra <note>A lustrum is a period of five years; ten lustra signifies fifty years of missionary work.</note>, the unwearied laborers of the Society of **JESUS** have toiled with the greatest effort and incredible zeal in cultivating this vast vineyard in every possible way—not without significant gain for souls—nevertheless, all of this must be considered too little if we look upon the wild desolation of the vineyard, which is still filled everywhere with thorns and briars. In this place, you may see as many monstrous institutions of various sects flourishing as there are districts of uncultivated parts; these are as full of curiosity as they are of superstition, and are the many foolish inventions of Demons, by which:

> ->*The wrath of the Demon, pressing upon the world with hatred and*<-
> ->*deceit,*<-
> ->*Always remains restless, never satisfied by the losses of men.*<-
<note>original Latin: "Daemonis ira premens odiis & fraudibus Orbem Semper, nec damnis hominum exfaturata quiescit."</note>

Hence the Neophytes <note>term: Neophytes (new converts to the Christian faith)</note>, who have already found the path of eternal Salvation through the irradiation of Heavenly light, are struck by such a great ruin of perishing souls. They turn to **YOU** as their supreme **FATHER** and the Moderator <note>term: Moderator (the Superior General or head of the Jesuit Order)</note> of the Society of **JESUS**, anxiously...

<summary>This dedication page describes the first fifty years of Jesuit missionary labor in a "vast vineyard," lamenting that despite their efforts, many regions remain dominated by superstitious sects and demonic influence. It highlights the concern of new converts for the souls of those still living in spiritual darkness.</summary>
<keywords>Jesuits, Society of Jesus, missions, evangelization, superstition, Neophytes, Superior General, spiritual warfare</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>The catchword "preci-" from the previous page likely introduces the "anxious and prayerful minds" mentioned in the opening sentence of this dedication.</meta>

# D E D I C A T I O N

<note>original: "Dedicatio"</note>

They flee with anxious and prayerful minds; to YOU the vows of afflicted souls are turned, as if to the sole restorer of spiritual losses. Just as you have already for many years performed the Apostolic duty of a preacher, you have illuminated the path of Gospel preaching with the highest applause of the entire City and the World <note>original: "Urbis & Orbis," a traditional reference to Rome and the wider world</note>, both by your voice and by the books you have published. Furthermore, you have proven through long experience what is harmful and what is beneficial to such a great multitude of Unbelievers. Thus, now that you have obtained the helm and the steering-oar <note>A nautical metaphor for leadership and governance</note> of this truly Apostolic Institution through Divine Providence, and having pitied the lot of unhappy souls, may you supply the appropriate remedies you have identified for bringing them into the fold of CHRIST. We hope this will be timely if, in the place of the senior Fathers who are gradually passing away, you continually substitute new and more new propagators of the Divine Law. This is necessary lest, as the Church gradually grows wild, the fruit of so many years—collected with such great labor and sweat by Our Fathers—be rendered void. In the meantime, it shall be our part, with continuous prayers poured out to GOD THE BEST AND GREATEST <note>original: "DEUM OPTIMUM MAXIMUM," a common honorific for God adapted from Roman titles for Jupiter</note>...

<summary>This dedication page praises a high-ranking ecclesiastical leader for his preaching and leadership, urging him to appoint new missionaries to preserve the Church's progress among non-believers.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, apostolic mission, preaching, evangelization, church leadership, divine providence, spiritual restoration</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page concludes the formal dedication of the work, likely following a sequence of praises for the patron's support of Jesuit missions. The catchword "PRO-" indicates the beginning of the "PROLOGUS" or "PROEMIUM" on the following page.</meta>

# DEDICATION.

I shall support **You** with prayers to the Heavenly Citizens <note>original: "Cœlites"; a poetic reference to the saints and angels in heaven</note>, so that those things which you have conceived—through the great capacity of **Your** mind—to be possible for the glory of the Divine Name and for the growth of the Chinese Church in those farthest ends of the Earth, you may at last accomplish with a distinguished heap of merits. Farewell, **Most Religious Father**, surviving long for **God, the Church,** and the entire **Society** <note>The Society of Jesus, or the Jesuits</note>.

->*Rome*, December 8th, in the Year 1666.<-

->*Of Your Most Religious Fatherhood*<-

->The most humble and obedient servant,<-

->**Athanasius Kircher.**<-

# PREFACE
## TO THE
# READER.

<image-desc>Large ornamental woodcut initial 'Q' featuring symmetrical floral scrolls and geometric patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

<margin>
The Fathers who contributed their efforts and urged the writing of this work.
</margin>
That which I promised long ago in the catalog of my books, I have now decided to fulfill by making good on my word. I was led to this plan primarily because, for about two decades <note>original: "duobus circiter lustris" — a lustrum was a five-year period</note>, I have gathered a vast abundance of materials concerning Chinese affairs and the antiquities and superstitions of neighboring kingdoms, in which those nations were reported to be miserably entangled. Of these materials, *Father Martino Martini of Trent*—that distinguished author of the *Chinese Atlas* <note>original: "Atlantis Sinici"</note> and formerly my private pupil in Mathematics—first communicated a significant collection. Just as he possesses a sharp, fervent, and wonderfully curious mind, well-trained in the mathematical disciplines for these tasks, so too has he omitted nothing of interest regarding the customs of those peoples or the nature of things, by which he might place grateful posterity—especially writers of Geography and Astronomy—under his debt. Thus, not content with merely inquiring into matters, he decided that what he investigated should be examined with his own eyes, and what he examined should be written down for the benefit of the Republic of Letters <note>The "Republic of Letters" refers to the long-distance intellectual community of scholars in the 17th century</note>, which he performed excellently in his *Atlas*. He was succeeded by *Father Michael Boym, a Pole*, who was sent to Rome to Pope Innocent X by the King and Emperor of the *Chinese, Constantine*, and his mother *Helena*—who were recently converted to the Christian faith through the work of *Father Andrew Xavier Koffler, an Austrian*. Father Boym contributed admirable things most worthy of the memory of posterity, as will be revealed in the course of this work. After him, *Father Philip Marino of Genoa*, the Procurator of *Japan*, arrived here. From his account—partly by word of mouth and partly from his book titled *On the Missions of the Fathers of the Society of Jesus undertaken in Japan, China, Tonkin, and other newly discovered Kingdoms*—I received many things indeed, which were wonderfully useful to me in decorating my "Sparta" <note>original: "spartam meam exornandam" — a classical idiom meaning to fulfill one's appointed task or province of study</note>. Following him came...

<summary>In this preface, the author introduces the origins of his work on China, citing the various Jesuit missionaries and scholars who provided him with firsthand geographical, cultural, and religious data.</summary>
<keywords>Martino Martini, Michael Boym, Philip Marino, China, Japan, Tonkin, Jesuit missions, cartography, Republic of Letters</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface to the work, introducing the primary sources and Jesuit travelers whose firsthand accounts form the basis of the following descriptions of Asia and China.</meta>

# Preface to the Reader.

While I was still residing here in **Rome**, two other German Fathers—the first being **Father Johann Grueber** from Linz, Austria, and the second **Father Heinrich Roth** from Augsburg, both men distinguished by their devotion to studies and learned through great experience in affairs—contributed such an abundance of rare materials to me that, in describing them, the vast quantity almost seems to have left me at a loss for words.

<margin>The journey of Father Johann Grueber.</margin>
<margin>Father Albert d'Orville dies at Agra.</margin>
**Father Johann Grueber**, renowned for his cultivation of mathematics, was about to depart for **China** in the year 1656. While he was here in **Rome**, I urged him to observe each and every thing with the appropriate faithfulness that might bring some light to the <term>Geographic Republic</term><note>A common 17th-century term referring to the international community of scholars and mapmakers.</note>. What I had so earnestly requested of him, he performed more faithfully than can be described. He departed from **Rome** in the year mentioned above and, having traveled through **Anatolia**, **Armenia**, **Persia**, **Hormuz**, **Cambay**, **India**, and finally all the kingdoms adjacent to the Ocean, he at last successfully reached **Macau**, the port of **China**. There, having somewhat restored his strength and measured the entire breadth of **China**, and having surveyed the principal cities, he finally reached the ultimate metropolis of **China**, **Beijing**, the royal seat of the Monarch of the **Chinese and Tartars**. Having stayed there for two years and observed everything with the greatest zeal, he was sent back to **Rome** by order of his superiors for the business of the Chinese Province, together with the **Venerable Father Albert d'Orville**. He measured the entire length of **Asiatic** land by a route hitherto unattempted by Europeans, and he set forth the wonderful and unknown things he observed. After the aforementioned **Father Albert d'Orville**—a man worthy of a longer life—died in the Kingdom of the **Mughals**, in the metropolis of **Agra**, broken by the labor of the journeys, Grueber took as his companion for the labors and the journey **Father Heinrich Roth**. Roth is a man conspicuous for his prudence and learning, who was then intent on promoting the Christian cause at **Agra**, and is most highly skilled in three languages: **Persian**, **Hindustani**, and **Sanskrit** <note>original: "Brachmanica"</note>. While I am writing these things, they are staying with me continually, and they do not cease to communicate those things which they know will in any way benefit the glory of the Divine Will and the advantage of the common good. However, since the Fathers are continually occupied in procuring the salvation of souls, they have neither the leisure, time, nor means to [describe] certain rare things which, in those vast [regions] of the world...

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page serves as the preface to the work, outlining the structure and purpose of the volume. It establishes the author's intent to preserve the findings of Jesuit missionaries in the East.</meta>

### Preface to the Reader.

Since they have observed these regions through the journeys they undertook, and since there is an abundance of information both to be described and brought to light, they have entreated this one thing of me: that those findings, discovered with such great labor and sweat, should not be surrendered to the moths and worms once committed to writing, but rather, gathered into a single volume, I should deliver them to the public light for the benefit of the Republic of Letters <note>The "Republic of Letters" refers to the long-distance intellectual community of scholars in the 17th and 18th centuries.</note>; which I have promised to perform in this Work. Truly, so that I might proceed with greater method, I have thought it best to divide it into six parts.

The first Part presents that marble monument, most celebrated throughout the whole World, for the sake of which this Work was begun by us. It was first discovered forty-five years ago in *China*, in a certain village of the Metropolitan City of *Xi'an* <note>original: "Siganfu"</note> (in which the orthodox doctrine, introduced into *China* about a thousand years ago by *Chaldean* <note>The author refers to the East Syriac or "Nestorian" Christian priests.</note> Priests and seen inscribed in *Syro-Chinese* characters) is explained with a full interpretation.

II. The second Part shows the various journeys undertaken to *China* by the preachers of the faith, from *St. Thomas the Apostle* continued down to these very times.

III. The third Part presents the origin of Idolatry born in those most remote ends of the earth; likewise concerning the diverse rites and abominable ceremonies in the worship of Gods, compared in parallel <note>original: "παραλλήλως"</note> to the rites of the *Egyptians*.

IV. The fourth Part explains the curious miracles of Art and Nature found in that vast Empire of the *Chinese* <note>original: "Sinarum"</note>, both regarding the location and the civil method of governing customary to the *Chinese*, as well as those things observed by our men in the triple Kingdom of Nature—that is, of minerals, plants, and animals; and at the same time, the histories published by our members concerning various exotic matters are defended from the quibbles of many.

V. The fifth Part presents the admirable aspects of Chinese architecture in the construction of Buildings, Bridges, Aqueducts, Walls, and other inventions unknown to *Europeans*.

VI. Finally, the sixth part treats at length of Chinese Letters <note>The Chinese writing system and characters.</note> and their structure. Supported therefore by the assistance of these Fathers, I have labored on these matters concerning the origin and Antiquities of the Oriental Kingdoms of *Asia*; if anything worthy of the Christian Republic should be found in them, I would wish you to ascribe it to them above all.

<meta>catchword: IN-</meta>

<summary>The author introduces a six-part compendium on China, highlighting the famous Nestorian Stele of Xi'an and the natural, cultural, and architectural wonders observed by Jesuit missionaries.</summary>
<keywords>Preface, China, Xi'an Stele, Nestorian Christianity, St. Thomas the Apostle, Idolatry, Egyptian comparison, Chinese architecture, Chinese characters, Asia</keywords>

### PART ONE.
->Interpretation of the Syrian-Chinese Monument.<-

**Chap. I.** The cause and occasion of this Work. Page 1.

**II.** A faithful, sincere, and literal interpretation of the Chinese-Chaldean <note>original: "Sinico-Chaldæi"</note> Monument, erected nearly a thousand years ago by the Gospel propagators of the Christian Law in a certain Kingdom of China called Shaanxi <note>original: "Xemſi"</note>, and finally discovered in the year 1625 to the great benefit of the Christian Law. 4.

**III.** Concerning the triple Interpretation of the Syrian-Chinese Stone or Monument. 10.

**IV.** Concerning the Cross carved on the very top of the Stone. 35.

**V.** Concerning the Articles of faith and other ceremonies and rites contained in the Monument. 37.

### PART TWO.
->Concerning the various Journeys undertaken to China.<-

**Chap. I.** By what means, and by whom, and by which routes, at different times, the Most Holy Gospel of Christ was carried into the farthest Regions of the East: India, Tartary, China, and the other Regions of Asia. P. 46.

**II.** Concerning the propagation of the Gospel by Saint Thomas the Apostle and his successors, carried out in all the Regions of Eastern Asia. 53.

# ATHANASIUS KIRCHER’S
## C H I N A
## I L L U S T R A T E D
### P A R T I.
## THE SYRO-CHINESE
### M O N U M E N T
### INTERPRETATION.
### C H A P T E R I.
->The cause and occasion of this Work.<-

<image-desc>An ornamental drop cap 'I' featuring intricate floral and foliate patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

<margin>The judgment of certain censors and critics regarding this Monument.</margin>
It is now about thirty years <note>original: "sex circiter lustra" — a lustrum was a five-year period in ancient Rome</note> since, in my *Coptic Forerunner* <note>referring to Kircher's 1636 book *Prodromus Coptus*</note>, I provided an exposition of a certain Syro-Chinese monument discovered in China in the year 1625. Although this earned no small applause among readers of a more sensible mind who were struck by the novelty of the matter, there was nonetheless no shortage of unfair censors and critics <note>original: "Aristarchi" — referring to Aristarchus of Samothrace, a famous harsh critic of Homer</note> who did not cease to attack it with witty sophistry. Indeed, they marked it with asterisks—though only dull and leaden ones—striving by every means to persuade not only themselves but everyone else that such a monument never existed in nature, and that it was a pure and simple fabrication of the Jesuits. Such people deserve to be regarded as those who, having rejected all faith in things divine and human, approve of nothing they have not seen themselves, and deem nothing believable unless their own brains have dictated it. They are like troublesome flies, flying toward any richness they encounter, never ceasing to obscure what is sincere and whole, or to stain what is pure with a foul and boastful breath. Among these was a certain modern writer who, to his own small honor, did not blush to attempt to shatter the truth of this monument with every effort and indeed with insolent mockery. He asserted that it was introduced by Jesuit fraud, a pure fabrication devised by the Jesuits both to deceive the Chinese and to extract treasures. I spare his name, partly out of Christian charity, and partly because he seemed unworthy of a response in the judgment of prudent and learned men. For the truth of this monument has been established and confirmed by the first-hand testimony <note>original: "αὐτοψίᾳ" (autopsia) — seeing with one's own eyes</note> of so many illustrious writers, and by so many books published throughout the Empire on this matter even by Chinese officials <term>Colais: high-ranking ministers or scholars in the Chinese imperial court</term> of great authority who were initiated into the Christian law, such as Leo and Paul. These works are displayed in the Museum of the Roman College to foreigners everywhere, together with the original copy of the monument sent from China...

<summary>Athanasius Kircher introduces his defense of the Nestorian Stele, a 7th-century Christian monument found in China in 1625, against critics who claimed the Jesuits forged it. He asserts the monument's authenticity based on eyewitness accounts and Chinese scholarly support.</summary>
<keywords>Athanasius Kircher, China Illustrata, Nestorian Stele, Syro-Chinese Monument, Jesuit missions, 1625 discovery, historical authenticity, Roman College Museum</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues Athanasius Kircher's defense of the authenticity of the Nestorian Stele (the "Sino-Syrian Monument") against critics who claimed it was a forgery. He establishes his intent to provide a definitive translation and geographical context for how Christianity reached China.</meta>

# ATHANASIUS KIRCHER

...and approved by the **Christian World**, he <note>Kircher refers to himself or the Church</note> cares little for what some secretive braggart <note>original: "Thraso clancularius," a reference to a boastful soldier character from Roman comedy, here used to insult a specific anonymous critic</note> barks from the farthest corner of the world. If that critic had abstained from slanders and insults, and had instead proposed the matter prudently as a doubt, he might have better served himself and his work. But it happens by the just judgment of God that those who strive to traduce the glory of the Divine Name with quibbles and sarcasms—against conscience and charity—usually suffer the shipwreck of their own name and labors in the end.

<margin>The doctrine delivered in the Monument is conformable to the orthodox Church.</margin>
Moved by these concerns, and lest a monument of such great importance for the faith should suffer the shipwreck of truth, I shall begin the thread of the story from its deepest roots. In this little work, I will strive to establish the truth of the undertaken Monument—confirmed not only by the eyes of our own Fathers who inspected it, but by the testimony of the Chinese themselves—with the help of Divine Grace, so that in the future no room for doubt remains. Thus, even the Heterodox <note>Non-Catholics, likely Protestants who doubted the stone's antiquity</note> will be forced to admit, from the interpretation of these Syrian-Chinese inscriptions, that nothing else was taught by the preachers of the Divine Word ten centuries ago that is not everywhere consistent and conformable with the orthodox doctrine of these times—indeed, it is the same. Therefore, the doctrine once scattered in **China** by Evangelical preaching is the very same which the universal Roman Catholic Church proposes to be believed today, as will be proven later by a manifold weight of arguments.

<margin>A double interpretation of this Monument.</margin>
So that the matter may be carried out in the desired order and method, I have decided to include, above all, a double interpretation of this stone, both complete and sincere. The first is translated word-for-word from a book printed in Chinese characters and explained by learned Chinese men themselves, providing the genuine pronunciation of the Chinese words as expressed by *Father Michael Boym* <note>original: "Michaële Boimo," a Polish Jesuit missionary and scientist</note>, who is most skilled in the Chinese language (as will be clear shortly from his *Letter to the Reader*). The second exposition avoids Chinese phrasing that is unfamiliar to our ears and is better suited for perceiving the genuine sense of the Tablet; I have added this along with scholarly notes <term>Scholia: explanatory comments or annotations</term> so that I might not seem to have omitted anything that could make this Monument more illustrious. 

Because someone might rightly doubt how and by what path those great promoters of the Christian Gospel, being of the Syrian-Chaldean nation, penetrated into those farthest shores of the earth, it seemed not only appropriate but entirely necessary for establishing the truth of the matter to shine a light for the perplexed reader through that dark labyrinth of various routes. I shall use the torch of this little work—that is, geographical reasoning—from which the reader will clearly understand (unless I am mistaken) that not only Christian doctrine, but also the superstitions and fables of the Chinese <margin>From where both the law of Christ and the superstitious fables of Ethnic doctrine came into China.</margin> originated before the coming of our Savior from those very same regions: namely Egypt, Greece, Syria, and Chaldea. 

As to where that vast Kingdom of the Chinese is located, into which our Monument teaches the Gospel penetrated, I shall point it out briefly rather than describe it at length. If the reader desires to know more of this, I refer him to the *Histories* of *Father Nicolas Trigault* and *Joannes Samedi*, as well as the *Atlas* of *Martino Martini* and countless others. There he will find most abundantly described the situation of China and its wonders, the nature and fertility of the regions, the multitude of great cities and men, and finally the political discipline, which seems to yield to no other Monarchy in the world. It shall be our task only to explain those things which are controversial or which leave readers wonderfully doubtful and perplexed regarding the ambiguity of names. I shall also bring to light in this work, as if in its proper place for the sake of the curious reader, the rarer secrets of hidden things observed in that Kingdom and others nearby—prodigies of both nature and art—which have not been touched upon by others.

<summary>Kircher announces his plan to prove the authenticity of the Nestorian Stele by providing two translations (one literal and one idiomatic) and explaining the geographical routes by which Syrian missionaries reached China.</summary>
<keywords>Nestorian Stele, authenticity, Michael Boym, Jesuit missions, China, Syrian-Chaldean, geography, religious continuity, Martino Martini, Nicolas Trigault</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introductory description of the Chinese Empire, establishing its geographical boundaries and the historical confusion regarding its various names.</meta>

# CHINA ILLUSTRATED.
A 2

<margin>Description of China.</margin>
**CHINA**, the greatest and most vast of all kingdoms, was placed by the Creator of the terrestrial globe at the furthest boundary of Asia. Thus, it remained entirely unknown to the ancients until the year 1220, when Marco Polo the Venetian <note>original: "M. Paulus Venetus"</note> first revealed it to us under the name of *Cathay* <note>original: "Catai"</note>, as will be shown later.

<margin>Boundaries of the Chinese Empire.</margin>
From the East, it is surrounded by the Eastern Ocean; to the North, it has Tartary adjacent to it, separated by a wall. Its boundaries in that direction, as yet unexplored, extend as far as the Icy Sea <note>The Arctic Ocean</note> and undoubtedly continue somewhere into North America, whether by the Strait or Isthmus of Anian <note>A semi-mythical strait believed at the time to separate Asia and North America, later identified as the Bering Strait</note>. However, to this very day—despite the great efforts and investigations of our Jesuit Fathers in China—the limits of these immense kingdoms and lands have been discovered by no one. To the West, it is surrounded partly by the ridges of very high mountains and partly by a sandy desert and other kingdoms to be reviewed shortly. Finally, to the South, it is bounded by the Southern Ocean and the kingdoms of Tonkin, Cochinchina, Laos, and others. Its latitude begins at the 18th degree and extends to the 43rd; that is, it stretches a distance of 1,440 Italian miles from South to North.

<margin>China is represented by its own people under the shape of a square.</margin>
From West to East, it consists of almost the same distance, which is why the Chinese represent it in their maps under the shape of a square. However, our Geographers, being equipped with a greater knowledge of Mathematics, describe it as having roughly the shape of a gibbous moon.

<margin>The diversity and confusion of names by which the Empire is distinguished.</margin>
As for the confusion of names, it is as great as the diversity of the neighboring nations. The Portuguese and Spanish call it China; the Ancients, as found in *Ptolemy*, called it Sin and Serica; the Arabs call it *Sin*, and the Saracens *Cathay*. Not only is none of these names commonly used among the Chinese themselves, but they are not even known by name. This is evident from the fact that it has been a custom of the Chinese from time immemorial that whenever the right of ruling passes from one family to another through the vicissitudes of human affairs, the Kingdom itself is marked with a new name by the one who begins to reign. The ruler who does this bestows some beautiful title upon the Kingdom according to his will and pleasure. Thus, we read that it was once called *Tan*, which is said to mean "spread without limit." At another time *Yu*, which indicates "rest." Then it was designated *Hin* under the name of a great Leader. I also find it called *Sciam*, as if you were to say, "the most ornate Kingdom." *Cheu* refers to a Kingdom that exceeds all remaining kingdoms in perfection. Also *Han*, a word by which they name the Milky Way. From this it happens that nearly every nation distinguishes it by its own different names. Today it is called either *Chung-kuo* <note>original: "Cium quo"</note> or *Chung-hua* <note>original: "Chium hoa"</note>. The first of these means "garden" <note>Kircher's interpretation here differs from modern etymology, which translates Chung-kuo as "Middle Kingdom"</note> because of the supreme pleasantness and abundance of things; the second means "middle," because the Chinese believe their Kingdom is situated in the middle of the world, which they likewise maintain is square.

<margin>Division of the Chinese Empire.</margin>
The whole Empire is divided into fifteen Kingdoms, separated by vast rivers and mountains, of which nine are counted in the Southern part and six in the Northern part. Nature intended this entire complex of Kingdoms to be, as it were, inaccessible to all. From the East and South, it is scarcely passable due to the violence of the ocean tides; from the West, the steep ridges of mountains offer no entry to anyone. From the North, it is fortified partly by a desert of immense vastness and partly by a wall of nine hundred Italian miles. This was built by King *Xio* <note>Qin Shi Huang</note> about two hundred years before the birth of our Savior, by the labor of a million men over a period of five years, to guard against the incursions of the Tartars. Thus armed with so many fortifications, it still stands by its own mass. If you consider the wall alone, it is a work full of wonder, which the Ancients would undoubtedly have numbered among the seven wonders of the World had they obtained knowledge of it. But let us list the names of the Kingdoms below.

<summary>This page introduces China's geography, its isolation from the ancient Western world, and the various names given to it by different cultures and dynasties. It highlights the Great Wall as a structural marvel and describes the Chinese perception of their land as a square "Middle Kingdom."</summary>
<keywords>China, Cathay, Marco Polo, Ptolemy, geography, Great Wall, Chinese dynasties, etymology, Chung-kuo, Tartars</keywords>

# THE WORKS OF ATHANASIUS KIRCHER

The Northern Kingdoms of the Chinese Empire are:
1. HONAN <note>original: "HONAM"</note>
2. SHAANXI <note>original: "XEMSI"</note>
3. SHANXI <note>original: "XANSI"</note>
4. SHANDONG <note>original: "XANTUM"</note>
5. BEIJING <note>original: "PECHIN"</note>
6. LIAODONG <note>original: "LEAUTUM"</note>

The Southern Kingdoms of the Chinese Empire are:
1. GUANGDONG <note>original: "CANTON" or "QUANTUNG"</note>
2. GUANGXI <note>original: "QUAMSI"</note>
3. YUNNAN
4. FUJIAN <note>original: "FUKIEN"</note>
5. JIANGXI <note>original: "KIAMSI"</note>
6. SICHUAN <note>original: "SUCHUEM"</note>
7. HUGUANG <note>original: "UTQUANG"</note>
8. ZHEJIANG <note>original: "CHEKIAM"</note>
9. NANJING <note>original: "NANKIM"</note>

But so that we may embrace all things summarily <note>original: "epitomicῶs" (Greek: briefly/concisely)</note>, we shall first place here a geographical diagram of the entire Empire divided into its fifteen Kingdoms, so that you may inspect the location of each more closely. And lest we seem to have omitted any memorable things regarding Cities, Mountains, Rivers, Lakes, and other things worthy of knowledge, we have introduced this geographical diagram which contains the description of all the Kingdoms; from this, as if from a single synopsis, you may find whatever occurs in the whole Empire that is worthy of consideration.

### CHAPTER II.

->*A faithful, sincere, and literal*<-

->INTERPRETATION<-

->*of the Sino-Chaldean Monument <note>The Nestorian Stele, a 7th-century stone tablet recording the arrival of Christianity in China.</note> erected nearly a thousand years ago by Gospel propagators of the Christian Law in a certain Kingdom of China called Shaanxi, and finally discovered in the year 1625 to the great benefit of the Christian Law.*<-

<margin>The inconstancy of human affairs.</margin>
**S**uch is the inconstancy of human affairs that, just as we see abundantly from the monuments of historians, all things are entangled in their own vicissitudes—turning from good to evil, from prosperity to adversity, from the heights to the depths, and from the best constitution of laws into the worst states of affairs, and back again—revolving and being revolved in a certain perpetual cycle <note>original: "pericyclosi"</note>. Thus also in this miserable catastrophe of affairs, we can promise ourselves nothing firm, stable, or solid; a fact which shines forth more clearly than the midday light, especially in the propagation of the faith. <margin>Various revolutions of the Christian law.</margin> It is not for me to describe here what vicissitudes Egypt, Greece, and finally all of Europe have suffered in the propagation of the Christian faith, since those things are known everywhere. We behold with groaning and sorrow that Churches which we read once burned with holiness, justice, and an incredible zeal for the faith, have now not only grown lukewarm, but—all orthodox faith having been removed—have perished, contaminated partly by idolatries, partly by the impure plague of the Mohammedan law, and finally partly by heretical depravity. Consequently, we ought not so much to investigate the hidden <margin>The concealed judgments of God.</margin> abyss of divine judgments regarding these matters, as to marvel at them above all else, and therefore work out our salvation with fear and trembling. God knows those whom He has chosen from all flesh; He knows those from the universal mass of the human race whom He has foreordained to eternal life. Just as a gardener gathers fruit of a better quality and already brought to maturity now from this tree and now from that, leaving and neglecting the rest, so the inscrutable depth of divine providence chooses from thousands a few whom He alone knows. And now from this corner of the earth, now from that, as if they were fruits destined for eternal life...

<meta>The text cuts off as the author prepares to discuss the "fruits" or converts gathered by providence.</meta>

<summary>Kircher lists the northern and southern provinces of China and introduces the Nestorian Stele, reflecting on the rise and fall of Christian communities throughout history as a result of divine providence.</summary>
<keywords>Provinces of China, Shaanxi, Nestorian Stele, Christian missions, Divine Providence, Church history, 1625 discovery</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory narrative of Athanasius Kircher's "China Illustrata," moving from general geographical descriptions to the history of Christianity in the Chinese Empire.</meta>

# CHINA ILLUSTRATED

<margin>The fervor of the first Christians in China and their decline.</margin>
God brings forth from his treasury the things reserved for his own times and places. This is evident from the very sowing of the Gospel, which was brought into the Kingdom of the Chinese either from the earliest beginnings of the Christian faith by the Holy Apostle Thomas <note>Kircher refers to the tradition that St. Thomas the Apostle traveled as far as China to preach.</note> (of whom more will be said later), or by his successors. Just as that Church burned with the highest fervor from the start and took on great growth in the gain of souls, so too, as that fervor gradually cooled—whether from the wickedness of men and an abominable luxury of life, or by the unlawful rites of diverse nations contrary to divine worship, or finally by the gradual lack of Pastors—it eventually grew wild. Wandering entirely from the path of Divine Law, it fell into a lamented abyss of enormous errors; until Divine Clemency, taking pity on the ruin of so many souls at the certain times which it reserved for itself, decided to restore the trampled hedge of the Vineyard (which had been exposed for so long to wild beasts) with new buds and vine-shoots through Apostolic men.

<margin>Four successions of the Christian faith in China.</margin>
It will be shown most amply in the following pages that this change of fortune occurred four times in the Kingdom of the Chinese. The last of these occurred when it pleased Him who placed times and moments in His own power, through the Religious of the Society of Jesus <note>The Jesuits.</note>, with an outcome more desired than hoped for (as the beginnings of great undertakings are most difficult). 

<margin>Difficult entry into China.</margin>
Entry into the Kingdom had been frequently attempted by various people, but was forbidden to all foreigners under the gravest penalties. But just as there is no wisdom or counsel against the Lord, so also by the ineffable disposition of His providence, it was brought about that entry was finally granted to our men—though not without the greatest labors, difficulties, and persecutions—with such fruit of souls that I would not say they could have hoped for more, but could not have even wished for it. While they were thus laboring strenuously in the Vineyard of the Lord, God the Best and Greatest was pleased to attest by a certain wonderful event how pleasing to Him was the doctrine they were spreading—inasmuch as it was the very same that other cultivators of the Christian Vineyard had introduced about a thousand years before. 

<margin>Divine providence in the resuscitation of the lost faith.</margin>
He chose to testify to this at that very time, so that by this means, as the heavenly doctrine emerged as if from a deep mist of darkness, we might not cease to admire the great increase of the Christian Church from the conversion of innumerable nations, and to sufficiently praise and proclaim the providence of the Divine Will in it. The matter is as follows:

<margin>Discovery of the Syro-Chinese Monument.</margin>
In the year 1625, while a trench was being dug in a certain village of *Siganfu* <note>Modern Xi'an.</note>, the metropolis of the Province of *Xensi* <note>Shaanxi.</note>, to find a suitable foundation for a building, it happened that during the excavation the diggers struck upon a stone slab remarkable for its Chinese writing. Having pulled it out, they immediately began to measure its size and found its length to be 9 1/2 of our palms, its width 5, and its thickness about one palm. 

<margin>The figure of the Cross at the top of the Monument.</margin>
At the top, it ended in a pyramid, on which a Cross was quite skillfully depicted. Those who have studied it carefully say the Cross spreads out at the corners in the manner of lilies, similar to the one seen at Meliapore on the tomb of the Holy Apostle Thomas, and not unlike that which the Knights of St. John of Jerusalem <note>The Knights Hospitaller.</note> wear, partly hung from the neck and partly sewn onto their clothes and cloaks. Beneath this Cross follows the title of the inscription, expressed in Chinese characters and idiom over the entire surface of the stone, as is clear from the attached figure. And just as the Chinese are drawn by a natural curiosity toward things full of wonder, so the first rumor of this discovered stone soon attracted a huge multitude of scholars flocking from all sides.

<margin>Curious Chinese.</margin>
<margin>The spreading of the news of the discovered Monument throughout the whole Empire.</margin>
Moved by the novelty of the matter, the Governor of the place, having contemplated the monument of venerable antiquity more deeply, wished to attract many from the whole Kingdom (which fame had already filled) to the city to celebrate the monument. He placed it upon a skillfully fashioned pedestal within a *Bonzio* <note>A Buddhist temple; from 'bonze', a term used by Europeans for Buddhist monks.</note>...

<sig>A 3</sig>

<summary>This page describes the historical decline and eventual rediscovery of Christianity in China, culminating in the 1625 unearthing of the Nestorian Stele (the Syro-Chinese Monument) in Xi'an.</summary>
<keywords>St. Thomas the Apostle, Jesuits, Xi'an, Shaanxi, Nestorian Stele, Syro-Chinese Monument, Christian missions in China, Knights of St. John</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the discovery of the Nestorian Stele (the Syro-Chinese Monument) in Xi'an, following the initial account of its excavation.</meta>

# ATHANASIUS KIRCHER

The precinct of the temple of the Bonzes <note>A term used by early travelers for Buddhist monks</note> had a roof placed over it, partly so that it would not suffer any damage from the ravages of time, and partly so that as many spectators as might come to see this unusual monument could read, examine, and describe it at their convenience.

<margin>Leo the Mandarin was the first to publish it.</margin>
And since characters unknown to the Chinese appeared to be mixed with the Chinese characters on the margins of the stone, people were held in anxious suspense to know what they signified. Their curiosity was finally fully satisfied by the diligence of our men, as will be made clear in what follows. The first of all was Leo the Mandarin <note>Li Zhizao (1565–1630), a high-ranking official and famous Christian convert</note>, already imbued with the Christian law; because of the immense benefit to the Christian faith which he hoped would emerge from it, he published an exact description of it to the entire Kingdom. Stirred by this, our Fathers also, as soon as they had read it, could not sufficiently admire the providence of the Divine Power, that in the time of the new Vineyard of Christ <note>The early Jesuit missions in China</note>, it pleased Him that a monument of such great importance for the conversion of the gentiles should emerge. Wherefore, unable to contain themselves, they traveled to *Siganfu* <note>Xi'an</note>, the great metropolitan city of *Xenfi* <note>Shaanxi</note>, to examine it with their own eyes. The first of these was

<margin>Father Alvaro Semedo was the first of our men to see and describe it.</margin>
Father Alvaro Semedo, a Portuguese, whose words I produce here all the more willingly because he was bound to me by a great bond of friendship while he acted as Procurator here in Rome, and he recounted to me by word of mouth everything he had observed regarding this Monument. He speaks as follows on *Page 158 in his History of Chinese Affairs, published in Italian:*

> <margin>Father Semedo was the first who saw the Monument excavated by the Chinese with his own eyes.</margin>
> *After three years, in 1628, some Fathers passed into 그that province on the occasion of a Christian Mandarin named Philip who was going there. These Fathers, by the authority of said Mandarin, established both a Church and a House in the metropolis of Siganfù; for blessed God, who willed that such a beautiful memory of the possession taken of His divine Law in that country should be discovered, also saw fit that its restoration in that same place should be facilitated.*
> *It was granted to me to be among the first, and I esteemed that stay happy for the opportunity of seeing the stone; having arrived, I cared for nothing else. I saw it, I read it, and I returned to read and gaze upon it at my leisure and at length; and considering its antiquity, I wondered how it could be so whole, and have the letters so clear and neatly carved. In its thickness <note>referring to the sides of the stone</note> it has many Chinese letters, which contain many names of the Priests and Bishops of that time. There are, however, many others which were not then recognized, because they are neither Hebrew nor Greek, but which, as I understand, contain the same names, so that if by chance some foreigner did not know how to read those of the country, he might perhaps understand the foreign ones. Passing later through Cochin, I arrived at Cranganore, the residence of the Archbishop of the coast, to consult about these letters with Father Antonio Fernandez of our Society, most expert in the literature of these Christians of St. Thomas; he told me they were Syriac letters, and those used there.* Thus says Father Alvaro Semedo.

<margin>Interpretation of the Syriac letters.</margin>
But since there was hardly anyone who had knowledge of the said letters, they were finally sent to Rome and came into my hands. I was the first, if I am not mistaken, to exhibit an interpretation of them in the *Coptic Prodromus* <note>Kircher's 1636 work on the Coptic language</note>, as will be shown more extensively later. However, I was not able to obtain all of them, as some were described on separate papers, perhaps excerpted from the original only to take a sample. But when, not long after, a complete copy of the original was sent to me—which even today is displayed for viewing in our Museum of the Roman College—then indeed, together with a more exact interpretation of both the Chinese and Chaldean <note>Syriac</note> characters, I decided that each and every part should be completed in this opportune place.

<margin>Father Martini mentions this in his Atlas.</margin>
Father Martino Martini, coming to Rome after Semedo, not only gave me an account of the Monument by word of mouth, but also mentions it extensively in his *Atlas*, in these words on *Page 44, where he treats the Province of Xenfi:*

# CHINA ILLUSTRATED

The province is active. What primarily makes this province famous is that most ancient stone, inscribed partly in Syriac and partly in Chinese characters, concerning the Divine Law brought to the Chinese by the successors of the Apostles. On it are read the names of the bishops and priests of that time, as well as the favors and indulgences granted by the Chinese Emperors. It also contains a brief but most exact and truly admirable explanation of the Christian law, composed in a most eloquent Chinese style. Regarding these matters, God willing, the second decade of the *Epitome of Chinese Histories* <note>A reference to Martino Martini's historical works on China</note> will provide much detail. This stone was found in the year 1625, when a trench was being dug in the city of Sianfu <note>original: "Samyun", likely a transcription error for Sianfu/Xi'an</note> to lay the foundations for a certain wall. The Governor of the place, having been informed of the discovery and having inspected the remains of venerable antiquity (of which the Chinese are most fond), immediately published a writing in praise of the monument. He caused the entire contents of the discovered stone to be engraved on another stone of the same size, preserving the same strokes of the characters and marks with due faithfulness. Our Fathers brought a prototype copy of this from there to Rome, where it is preserved today in the Museum of the Roman College. The shape of the stone is an extended parallelogram, five palms wide, one thick, and nearly ten long. At the top is a Cross, much like that of the Knights of Malta. If anyone desires a more accurate knowledge of this, he will find the whole matter explained and deduced more extensively in the *Coptic Prodromus* by the Reverend Father Athanasius Kircher, and in the *Relation of China* by the Reverend Father Alvaro Semedo. This knowledge of the Law of God, which had been obliterated and deleted from human memory by a very long interval of time, the Fathers of our Society have restored anew, with many temples erected to the living and true God and administered with the highest piety. Two of our Fathers there strenuously cultivate that new Vineyard of the Lord; one of them has fixed his seat in the Metropolis itself, while the other travels here and there through the Province to promote the worship of God and the good of souls, wherever greater necessity demands, so that he may break and share the bread of salvation with all those everywhere who seek it. Thus says Father Martino Martini.

<margin>Father Michael Boym</margin>
To these accounts was finally added that of *Father Michael Boym*, who brought me a more exact relation of this monument than all others. He corrected all the defects in its description using a Chinese manuscript which I have in my possession. He completed, in my presence, a new and detailed word-for-word interpretation of the entire tablet, a task begun with the help of his companion Andrew Don Sin, a native of China and most skilled in his mother tongue. He wished all these things to be attested in the following letter given to the Reader, in which he exactly described the sequence of the whole affair and whatever else occurred worthy of consideration regarding this monument. With his consent, I have decided to prefix this letter to this interpretation as a clear testimony of the truth for the eternal memory of the matter. I have also seen to it that the stone monument be engraved according to the original brought from China, which survives in my Museum to this day, with its genuine marks and characters, both Chinese and Chaldean <note>Kircher uses "Chaldean" to refer to Syriac script</note>, with scholia <note>explanatory notes</note> added. The said letter of Father Michael Boym follows.

# OF ATHANASIUS KIRCHER

> "...had obtained the signatures of all the Priests to be interpreted from China: it was therefore decided to faithfully publish the entire script itself, both in Chinese characters and in Latin, with a word-for-word Latin translation, retaining the Chinese phrasing, and also to present to everyone all the Syriac signatures of the Syrians, commented upon by the most skilled interpreter of languages, Father Athanasius Kircher, along with other Chinese [signatures], as most brilliant witnesses of the Catholic Truth. To put it in a single word, [the goal was] to expose the Stone itself to the eyes of the whole world in the Chinese idiom, just as it was carved in the year of Christ 782; so that from this most ancient testimony anyone might conclude how true the modern doctrine of Catholics is, since that same doctrine was clearly preached in the opposite world among the Chinese a thousand years ago, namely, in the year 636 from the birth of Christ.
<margin>The original of the Stone is kept in Rome in the Museum of the Roman College.</margin>
> The Chinese original of the Stone is kept in Rome in the Museum of the Roman College of the Society of Jesus <note>The Jesuits</note>, and another copy is in the Archive of the Professed House. I myself also brought a book from the Chinese Doctors and most grave Magistrates, printed in Chinese at the very time the Stone had been found, in which the writing of the Stone is exhibited, expressed most truly and exactly according to the truth of its archetype.
> In the 'Armored Prefaces' <note>original: "Prologis Galeatis" — a term for a defensive or introductory preface</note> attached to the book, they exhort all Chinese to finally approach the 'Masters of the Great West' (as they call the Fathers of the Society of Jesus) and see that they preach exactly the same Law among the Chinese which their ancestors, along with so many Emperors, had embraced ten centuries ago; and which the Chinese books printed by the Fathers of the Society of Jesus had exhibited before the Stone was found. It remains now for us to explain briefly how that marble Stone was discovered.
<margin>*San-tiano.</margin>
> When, after the death of Saint Francis Xavier on the island of *San*chêu <note>Shangchuan Island</note> in China, the venerable Father Matteo Ricci and other Fathers of the Society of Jesus had brought the Gospel of Christ into the interior of China, and had erected Residences and Churches in several Provinces, so that the propagation of the holy faith had made no small progress in the Province of *Xen-sy* <note>Shaanxi</note>; in the year of Christ 1625, one of the
<margin>Discovery of the Stone.</margin>

He wrote (which other Fathers, who later established a Residence and Church in *Sỹ-ngan fù*, affirmed along with the Christians and the heathens themselves) that the
<margin>Figure and form of the Stone.</margin>
> Stone found was five palms wide, one palm thick, and nine or more palms long; the top of the Stone was formed like an oblong pyramid of two palms [high] and one wide; on the summit, a Holy Cross carved with a chisel above small clouds, which imitated lilies with its arms, was depicted. Besides the Chinese script, on the left side and below, the Syriac names of the Syrian Priests are seen, and also other Chinese names of the same Priests added underneath. (It is the custom for the Chinese to have several names; whence even today Christians retain both the name of the Saints received in Baptism and another Chinese name.) The Governor of the place, informed of the monument found, struck by both the novelty of the thing and also by an omen (for on that very day his son had died), ordered an elegant composition in praise of the discovered monument to be made and engraved on another similar marble Stone; he took care to place both in a temple of the Bonzes <note>Buddhist monks</note> called *Taù Sù*, one mile distant from the walls of the Metropolis *Sỹ-ngan-fù*, for a perpetual memory of the matter.

# ATHANASIUS KIRCHER

> "...to speak most accurately, nothing can be certainly deduced regarding this from the remains found; the aforementioned evidence can only prove that those Priests were sent from the Church of St. Thomas, or from Babylon—which then governed the Malabar Christians of the *Serra* <note>original: "Serra" - a term used by the Portuguese to refer to the mountainous regions of Malabar, India</note> (as the Portuguese later discovered)—to establish an Episcopal See and introduce the faith to the peoples of China. This is likely attested by the ancient Syriac language itself, *Estrangelo* <note>A classical form of the Syriac alphabet</note>, which is retained in that Church and was formerly in use in Babylon and Syria. Furthermore, the prayers composed in praise of St. Thomas can be well understood in this way: namely, that through the merits of the Holy Apostle, and from his Church, the Priests perhaps first of all brought the faith which they had received from St. Thomas into China; therefore, the credit for their conversion is rightly and deservedly attributed to St. Thomas. Wherefore, since no mention of St. Thomas the Apostle is found in the traces of the preached faith detected thus far, we cannot assert with certainty that Saint Thomas, or another Apostle, preached the Gospel to the Chinese. However, many centuries later, there were Christians 
<margin>Christians under Prester John, worshippers of the Cross.</margin>
of *Prester* John <note>A legendary Christian patriarch and king popular in European chronicles</note> (whom *Marco Polo the Venetian* calls *Unc Can*), who I believe were called Worshippers of the Doctrine of the Cross, and who penetrated into China with the Tartars, or a little before. For those from Judea—or rather Syro-Chaldea or Malabar—being much older than the Christian Tartars, also wished their followers to be called disciples of the *Luminous Doctrine* <note>original: "Kin Kiao" (Jingjiao), the Chinese name for the Nestorian Christian faith</note>. I would believe, however, that those Preachers did not come from Judea; for the Syriac language and names testify that they were Syrians. Yet they said they were from Judea because they preached the Law which had its origin in Judea. But from exactly which place those Syrian Priests came, as well as their Syriac signatures, we leave to the industrious commentaries of *Reverend Father Athanasius Kircher*, a man who has served the study of antiquity most excellently. So that he might complete this with greater faith and solidity, we have offered for this purpose the same text, excerpted from a book (which had already been printed by Chinese Doctors, men of the highest authority and faith, and circulated throughout the Empire). This was transcribed in Chinese characters with fresh labor by his own hand by the noble Chinese youth *Don Andrew Chin*, the inseparable companion of my journey to the Apostolic See in Rome; it was translated by me word-for-word into Latin and marked with numbers. The book, which is entirely consistent with the original Stone, we have placed in his Museum—a sort of storehouse of rare things—for a perpetual memory of the matter, together with an attestation of the fact signed by my own hand and those of others originating from China."
"Rome, November 4, Year 1653."

## CHAPTER III.

### Concerning the Triple Mode and Method of Interpretation
### *Note to the Reader.*

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'A' containing a landscape scene with a house and trees.</image-desc>Before we proceed to the exact exposition of our Monument, we have judged it necessary to explain the triple method of interpretation presented here, lest the reader, perplexed by so many divisions of words and names marked with their own numbers, should remain hesitant or doubtful.

The first Interpretation, therefore, teaches nothing other than the manner in which the characters...

<meta>This page continues the introductory material for the translation of the Nestorian Stele, a famous 8th-century Christian monument found in China. The author, Athanasius Kircher, is explaining the methodology of his three-fold translation system.</meta>

<page-num>11</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>CHINA ILLUSTRATED.</header>

how the names and words contained in the Table of the Monument ought to be pronounced in Latin <note>original: "Latinè pronunciari debeant"; Kircher uses Latin phonetics to approximate Chinese sounds for European readers</note>.

Interpretation II, the verbal one, indicates what the characters of the names and words contained in the Monument properly signify.

Interpretation III contains a certain paraphrastic exposition of the Monument, by which the Chinese phrasing has been avoided as much as possible—since it is unfamiliar to European ears—and the hidden meanings of the Table, translated into the Latin language, are explained a little more at length.

The Reader should therefore note: That, just as the entire context of the Chinese writing on the Monument is divided into 30 columns, so also we have decided that the following Table should be divided into the same number; in such a way that the Chinese words expressed in Latin letters correspond as exactly as possible to those contained in the columns of the Monument, according to the natural arrangement of the numbers. We have done this for this reason: so that the curious Reader might immediately recognize the way of pronouncing each Chinese character from the correspondence of the numbers. For example: If you desire to know how this Chinese character 月 <note>The character for "Moon" or "Month"</note>, which is contained in the third position of Column 10 of the Table, should be pronounced in Chinese: you will do thus; seek Column 10 in the following Table of Interpretation I, and the third place in the same offers the Chinese word (*Chun yue*) <note>original: "Chun yue"; likely a phonetic rendering of the lunar month</note> and thus the Chinese character 月 is to be pronounced. Again, if you desire to know how this character 王 <note>The character for "King" or "Lord"</note>, which occupies the fourth place in Column 7, should be pronounced; seek Column 7 in the following Table, and the 4th place in it will give the Chinese word (*Sem*) expressed in Latin; which is the genuine pronunciation of the character 王. Know that you must proceed in no other way for each and every word of the Chinese Table, by comparing them with the words of the Latin Table according to the series of numbers.

---

<margin>Interpretation of the meaning</margin>
If, however, anyone should desire to know not only the way of pronouncing, but also the meaning of the Chinese character—for example, 月—he shall go to the Table of Interpretation II (the verbal one), and in the third place of Column 10, it will offer the word (*Moon*), which is the meaning of the said character. Likewise, in Column 7, the fourth place will give the word (*life*) <note>original: "vita"; Kircher's interpretation of the character 王 here as "life" or "living" reflects his specific theological reading of the stele</note> which is the true meaning of the character 王. And thus you shall proceed in the same manner for all the rest; we have thought these things should be suggested to the Reader in this place, so that the meaning of the words marked with so many numbers would not be hidden from him. You see, therefore, that in the Chinese Table there are 29 columns, besides the title column marked 0; each of which we have marked with their numbers in order for some distance, according to the natural series of numbers. However, the fact that all are not marked with numbers all the way to the bottom of the column was done for this reason: first, so that we would not clutter the columns with so many numbers; and second, because we hoped that the Reader would easily find the location of the desired character by himself. For example: if anyone should wish to know how this character 大 <note>The character for "Great"</note>, which occurs in Column 18 without a number, is pronounced; he shall proceed from the beginning of Column 18 by the order of numbers until the said character, and he will find it placed in the 9th position. This, therefore, found in the 9th place of Column 18 in the following Table, will give (*Ta*) as the pronunciation of the character 大, and in Interpretation II, the 9th place of Column 18 will simultaneously give its meaning, which is (*established*) <note>original: "instituit"</note>. If, however, two characters marked with the same numbers should occasionally occur in the Table, it indicates a certain amplification, which the Writer expressed with two characters for the reason that they nevertheless signify one thing in substance.

It remains for us to explain the vowels or accents with which the Chinese words in the following Table are marked. The idiom of the Chinese is very limited; hence, just as by a multitude of letters all...

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Chinese language, specifically addressing the relationship between its limited spoken vocabulary and its vast written character system.</meta>

# THE WRITINGS OF ATHANASIUS KIRCHER

Just as [the Chinese language] surpasses other nations of the world in other respects, it also yields to them all in the scarcity of its words. For the number of their words scarcely exceeds one thousand six hundred. Furthermore, all words end in vowels, and the few deprived of this termination mostly end in *m* and *n*. They are all monosyllabic and indeclinable, both nouns and verbs alike. For this reason, the language is rendered highly equivocal <note>meaning ambiguous or having multiple meanings</note>, so that often a verb is taken in place of a noun, and a noun in place of a verb.

Yet it might not undeservedly seem a wonder to anyone how so many and such great characters—which in their lexicon called the *Haipien* <note>original: "Haipien," likely referring to the *Haipian* or a similar large dictionary</note>, that is, the "Great Sea," number up to sixty thousand—can be applied to such a small number of words as we have mentioned. To make this clear, it must be known that Chinese words, as we showed a little earlier, scarcely exceed 1,600. Examples of these are, for instance: *Ca, cau, can; Ce, che, chi, chim; Ci, co, chu; Fe, fo, fi,* etc., as is evident from their dictionary. 

But just as each of these words has very different meanings, they likewise cannot be understood except by a different accent. Therefore, accents are necessary both for writing and speaking to understand words of this kind. Hence, to make the Chinese language easier, our Reverend Fathers <note>referring to the Jesuit missionaries</note> have arranged the ascent and descent of the Chinese accents to be observed in pronunciation according to the musical notes **UT, RE, MI, FA, SOL, LA**. 

Thus, for example, the single word *Ya* written in Latin letters, when marked with five notes, expresses different meanings; it must be pronounced with different tones and accents just as it is expressed by the Chinese with different characters. The vowels <note>here meaning the tonal marks</note> are those which follow:

->Λ — \ / ∪ ◞ ◟<-

The first mark Λ corresponds to the musical accent **UT**: the Chinese sound or enunciation, which they call *Cho pîm* <note>original: "Cho pîm," likely *Zhuoping*, the level tone</note>, signifies the same as a first voice coming forth level.

The second mark — corresponds to the musical **RE**, and is called in Chinese *Pim xim* <note>original: "Pim xim," likely *Ping sheng*</note>, as if you were to say a clear, level voice.

The third mark \ corresponds to the musical **MI**, and is called by the Chinese *Xám xim* <note>original: "Xám xim," likely *Shang sheng*</note>, that is, a high voice.

The fourth mark / corresponds to the musical **FA**, and in Chinese signifies *Kiú xim* <note>original: "Kiú xim," likely *Qu sheng*</note>, the high voice of one departing.

The fifth mark ∪ corresponds to the musical **SOL**, in Chinese *Gĕ xim* <note>original: "Gĕ xim," likely *Ru sheng*</note>, that is, the hurrying voice of one entering. The remaining two accents ◞ and ◟ indicate a flat voice.

In this manner, as we hinted a little earlier, this monosyllabic word *Ya* is indifferent in itself, but according to the diversity of the marks by which it is distinguished, it expresses different meanings, as follows:

->*Yâ*, God.<-
->*Yā*, Mute.<-
->*Yà*, Excellent.<-
->*Yá*, Amazement.<-
->*Yă*, Goose.<-

We deemed it necessary to set all these things forth here so that the reader would not be unaware of what is signified by the many marks with which the words of the following Chinese Table are distinguished. But having now premised these things, let us begin the interpretation of the truly admirable Stone <note>referring to the Nestorian Stele</note>.

<summary>Kircher explains the tonal nature of the Chinese language, comparing its five tones to musical notes to help Western readers understand how a single syllable like "Ya" can have multiple meanings. This serves as a linguistic primer before he presents his translation of the Nestorian Stele.</summary>
<keywords>Chinese language, linguistics, tones, musical notation, Jesuit missionaries, Haipien dictionary, monosyllables, Nestorian Stele</keywords>

# Memorial of the Propagation in China of the Luminous Religion from Daqin
<note>Daqin was the Chinese name for the Roman Empire or the Near East; "Luminous Religion" (Jingjiao) is the name used by these early Christians, often identified as Nestorians.</note>

<note>The following text corresponds to the central grid of Chinese characters, transcribed column by column from right to left.</note>

**Column 0** Behold the unchangeably true and still, the first of the first and without origin; the far-reaching and spiritually void, the last of the last and mysteriously existing. He who holds the mysterious
**Column 1** axis of the universe and created all things, the one who is more venerable than all the holy ones: is this not our Three-One <note>The Trinity</note> mysterious body, the unoriginated True Lord,
**Column 2** Aloho? <note>original: "阿羅訶" (A-luo-he), a phonetic rendering of the Syriac 'Alaha' for God</note>. He determined the four quarters of the world by the sign of the Cross and moved the primordial winds to produce the two forces <note>Yin and Yang</note>. Darkness and the void were changed, and
**Column 3** the heavens and earth opened; the sun and moon revolved and day and night were made. As a master craftsman, He fashioned all things and established the first man. To him, He gave a special
**Column 4** goodness and harmony, commanding him to rule over the vast ocean. His original nature was humble and not prideful; his heart was pure and simple, originally without
**Column 5** excessive desires. But then Satan spread his delusions, adding a glittering exterior to that pure essence. He introduced a wedge of "peace and security"
**Column 6** into the midst of goodness, and pushed man into the abyss of darkness. Consequently, three hundred and sixty-five sects followed
**Column 7** one after another, competing to weave the nets of their laws. Some pointed to material objects as the source of faith; some fell into the duality of "being" and "non-being"; some
**Column 8** offered prayers and sacrifices to seek blessings; some boasted of their goodness to deceive others. Their intellects labored anxiously, and their emotions were enslaved.
**Column 9** They gained nothing for their aimless efforts and were scorched by their own distress. Accumulating darkness, they lost their way and eventually strayed from the right path. Thereupon,
**Column 10** our Three-One divided His body, the Luminous Lord Messiah <note>original: "彌施訶" (Mi-shi-he)</note>, veiling His true majesty and appearing in the world as a man. Spirits and
**Column 11** heavens proclaimed the joy; a Virgin gave birth to the Holy One in Daqin. A bright star announced the auspicious event; Persians saw the glory and came with
**Column 12** tributes. He fulfilled the old laws spoken by the twenty-four holy ones <note>The Old Testament prophets</note> and governed families and nations according to the Great Way. He established the
**Column 13** new teaching of the silent operation of the Three-One Pure Wind <note>The Holy Spirit</note>, forming good character through right faith. He set the standard of the eight boundaries <note>The Beatitudes</note>, refining dross
**Column 14** into truth. He opened the gate of the three constants <note>Faith, Hope, and Charity</note>, bringing life and destroying death. He hung up the sun of mercy to break into the chambers of darkness, and the devil's delusions
**Column 15** were thereupon all destroyed. He rowed the galley of mercy to ascend to the palace of light, and all sentient beings were thereby saved. His
**Column 16** work of power being finished, at high noon He ascended to the Truth. He left behind twenty-seven books <note>The New Testament</note> to expand the great transformation and open the secret of the soul.
**Column 17** The ritual of water and wind washes away vain glory and cleanses the heart to a pure whiteness. As a seal, we hold the Cross, which sheds its light to the four quarters and unites all.
**Column 18** We strike the wood <note>a sounding board</note> to echo the sounds of benevolence and mercy; we worship toward the East to hasten on the path of life and glory. We grow beards to show
**Column 19** our outward practice, and shave our crowns to show we have no inward passions. We do not keep slaves, but treat the noble and the humble as equals. We do not hoard
**Column 20** riches and wealth, but show ourselves to be completely selfless. Our fasts are made perfect by retreat and meditation; our prohibitions are made firm by silence and
**Column 21** restraint. Seven times a day we offer praise, a great protection for both the living and the dead. Once every seven days we offer a sacrifice <note>The Eucharist</note>, washing the heart and returning to purity. This
**Column 22** True and Eternal Way is wonderful and difficult to name; its merits and use are so brilliant that we are forced to call it the Luminous Religion. But the Way without the Sage
**Column 23** is not spread; the Sage without the Way is not great. When the Way and the Sage are in harmony, the world becomes civilized. The Emperor Taizong, the Cultured
**Column 24** Emperor, began his reign in glory and splendor, ruling over the people with wisdom and holiness. In the country of Daqin there was a man of high virtue named Alopen <note>original: "阿羅本"</note>,
**Column 25** who, observing the blue clouds and carrying the true scriptures, braved difficulties and dangers by following the winds. In the ninth year of the Zhenguan era <note>635 AD</note>, he
**Column 26** arrived at Chang'an. The Emperor sent his Minister of State, Duke Fang Xuanling, to lead an escort to the western suburbs to welcome the guest into the palace.
**Column 27** The scriptures were translated in the Library; the Emperor inquired about the Way in his private apartments. Deeply convinced of its truth and correctness, he gave special orders for its propagation. In the
**Column 28** twelfth year of Zhenguan, in the autumn, in the seventh month, the edict stated: "The Way has no constant name, and the Holy has no constant form. Teachings are established according to the locality,
**Column 29** secretly benefiting all living beings. Alopen, a man of great virtue from the country of Daqin, has brought scriptures and images from afar to offer them at our capital."

<note>The transcription of the central text ends here on this facsimile, though the original monument continues with the history of the mission through the 8th century.</note>

<note>This table represents Kircher's attempt to map the sounds of the Chinese characters found on the Nestorian Stele into a Romanized phonetic system. The diacritics (accents like ̂, ̆, and ́) were used by Jesuit missionaries to denote the specific tones and vocal qualities of the Chinese language, which was a revolutionary linguistic effort in the 17th century.</note>

<margin>Column 28.</margin>
28. He illuminated and revived the World, he gave laws to a hundred barbarians; the Law alone responds most amply and uniquely to perfection; compelled, we shall speak its name: it is the Law of the Trinity in Unity. The Emperors knew how to act, the Vassal ought to report it. I erect this most flourishing Stone as a monument of eternal praise for the original felicity of the great Empire of the Chinese, in the second year of the Royal family of the Tam Emperor Kien chun, when it was the beginning of Autumn, on the seventh day of the month, on the day of the great light and most flourishing ornament (that is, Sunday), this Stone was erected.

29. Chao y lam, who previously had been Tay chen su su çan Kim. The official called Liu fieu wrote this with his own hand.
