<meta>This page continues the account of Jesuit exploration into Central Asia, specifically focusing on the overland routes to China (Cathay) and the travels of Benedict Goës and Antonio de Andrade.</meta>

# OF ATHANASIUS KIRCHER

<margin>*Iolci. Hancialix.*</margin>
Having begun his delay anew, Benedict <note>Benedict Goës (1562–1607), a Jesuit explorer who traveled overland from India to China.</note> first reached the *Iolci-Customs House* of the Kingdom; from here, in a journey of 25 days, he traversed the following places: *Hancialix, Alceghet, Hagabathet, Egriar, Mesetelec, Thalec, Horma, Thoantac, Mingieda, Caperalcol, Zilan, Sarogne betal, Cambaso, Aconfersec, Ciacor, and Acsu.*

<margin>*Acsu through the desert. Gucia in 20 days. Pucian. Turphan. Camul in 9 days. The walls of the Chinese Kingdom. Socieu.*</margin>
*Acsu* is a town in the Kingdom of *Cascar* <note>Kashgar</note>, from which he set out on a laborious journey through the desert called *Caracatai*—that is, "Black *Cataia*" <note>Kara-Khitan</note>. He passed through *Oitograch, Gazò, Casciani, Dellai, Saregabadal, Ugan*, and finally reached *Gucia*. Departing from here, he arrived after a 25-day journey at the city of *Cialis*, which is under the jurisdiction of the King of *Cascar*. There, Saracens <note>A common historical term for Muslims</note> returning from the previous year's caravan from *Cataio*—that is, *Peking*, the Royal city of the Chinese—told our Benedict wonderful things about Father *Matteo Ricci* and his companions. Consequently, our traveler was amazed to find that he had discovered *Cataium* <note>Cathay</note> at the very first location in China. From here, in 20 days, he reached *Pucian*, a town of the same kingdom, and from there *Turphan* and *Aramuth*, and finally arrived at the fortified city of *Camul* <note>Hami</note>. Nine days after *Camul*, he finally reached the long-desired walls of the Northern Chinese Kingdom at a place called *Chiaicum* <note>Jiayuguan</note>. Having been admitted within the walls, after a one-day journey, he entered *Socieu* <note>Suzhou, now Jiuquan</note>, the first Chinese city. He found no other "Cathay" besides China; thus, he henceforth laid aside every doubt regarding the true location of Cathay, which is commonly confused with China by the Saracens.

Note, however, that this journey toward the North from *Lahore* was drawn out longer than necessary, since he could have reached his destination by a much shorter route from *Lahore*. But just as this route through the mountains of *Tibet* had not yet been discovered, so too was he forced to attempt the route through *Uzbek* and *Samarkand*, which was more common at that time—even though it was a detour through great winding paths and notorious everywhere for robberies—in order to accommodate the customs of the merchants.

<margin>*The Kingdom of Uzbek.*</margin>
The Kingdom of *Uzbek* is extended from West to East by a vast distance of regions, composed of three very large kingdoms: the first is the Kingdom of *Samarkand*, famous for the birth of *Tamerlane*; the second is called *Tarphan*; the third *Turphan*. All are infamous for the laws of *Mohammed*. The men are cruel, robbers, bloodthirsty, and mortal enemies of Christians, just as histories record of the *Scythians*. For this reason, that kingdom is now entirely impassable to Christians, except perhaps for those who, having denied Christ, wish to adhere to the impious *Mohammed*. And this is the route from *Uzbek* into *Cathay*.

<margin>*The journey of Father Andrade. From Lahore to Srinagar, Chaparangue. The mountain of the Ganges and Indus. Redoc. Maranga. Tanchut. China.*</margin>
The journey which Father *Antonio de Andrade*, a Portuguese, undertook into the Kingdom of *Tibet* is as follows: Crossing the Ganges from *Lahore*, he first came to *Srinagar* and *Chaparangue* <note>Tsaparang</note>, huge and very populous cities. Crossing from these over a very high mountain, he observed at its summit a huge lake, which is the common *hydrophylacium* <note>term: hydrophylacium: A subterranean or high-altitude reservoir believed to feed major rivers.</note> of the *Indus*, the *Ganges*, and the other major rivers of India. From here, after a journey of many days likewise through high mountains, he reached *Redoc*, a very cold Northern region, and the city of the same name. From there, through the Kingdom of *Maranga* and the *Tangut* Kingdom of the Tartars, *Cataio*—that is, *China*—can easily be reached in a space of two months.

### §. II.

->Another journey from China into Mogor <note>The Mughal Empire</note> accomplished by<-
->our Fathers, Fr. Albert d'Orville<-
->and Fr. Johann Grueber. Regarding which<-
->see the Map.<-

<margin>*Journey from Peking to the city of Siningfu, located at the walls.*</margin>
1. These Fathers, therefore, set out from *Peking* in the year 1661, in the month of June. They completed the journey to *Xi'an* in thirty days, and from there to *Sining* or *Siningfu* in about the same number of days, having twice crossed the yellow river, which they call the *Hoang*. *Sining*, or *Siningfu*, is a large and populous city built at those vast walls of the Kingdom of the Chinese, through whose gate the first entrance into *Cathay* or *China* lies open to merchants from *India*, where also...

# CHINA ILLUSTRATED

<page-num>65</page-num>

they are compelled to stay until further permission to enter is granted by the King. This city is situated at a latitude of 36 degrees and 20 minutes.

<margin>Journey from Sining through the Kalmyk desert to the beginning of the Kingdom of Lhasa.</margin>
2. From *Sining*, they arrived after a three-month journey through the desert of *Kalmyk Tartary* at the beginning of the Kingdom of *Lhasa*, which the *Tartars* also call *Barantola*. The desert is partly mountainous and partly level, strewn with grit and sand, and utterly sterile and unfruitful; however, nature has occasionally provided it with several streams, whose banks provide a sufficient abundance of green herbs and pastures for the beasts of burden. Furthermore, as this desert takes its origin from the innermost midlands of *India*, and is likewise extended straight from the South to the North, no one has been found to this day who has explored its limit. Many think it extends as far as the frozen sea <note>The Arctic Ocean</note>, about which more is said in my work *The Subterranean World*. It has, however, various names.
<margin>The Sandy Desert and its various names.</margin>
*Marco Polo the Venetian* calls that desert *Lop*. It is infamous for the diabolical illusions and the multitude of ghosts appearing everywhere; however, our Fathers mention nothing of this, since the fact that such ghosts appeared once or twice does not prove a perpetual continuation of them appearing to everyone at all times. The *Tartars* formerly called the desert *Belgian*, now *Samo*, or the *Chinese* call it *Kalmyk*; others call it *Kara-Khitai*, that is, *Black Cathay*, where, except for *Wild Bulls* <note>likely Yaks</note> of enormous size, you will find no other animal. Yet the *Tartars*, accustomed to deserts, wander through it at all times; they also establish their *Hordes* wherever they find a stream suitable for pasture. The *Hordes of the Tartars* are huts fit for receiving men and livestock, as the Figure indicates.

<margin>29 degrees latitude.</margin>
3. From *Lhasa* or *Barantola*, situated at 29 degrees and 6 minutes latitude, they came in four days to the foot of Mount *Langur*. *Langur* is
<margin>The very high mountain called Langur.</margin>
the highest mountain of all, so that on its summit travelers can hardly breathe on account of the thinness of the air; nor can it be crossed in the summer time without manifest danger to life, because of the poisonous exhalations of certain herbs. Because of the horrendous precipices and rocky tracts, neither a carriage nor a beast of burden can pass through this mountain, but the whole journey must be completed on foot, in a space of nearly a month, as far as <margin>Kuti, a city of the kingdom of Nepal.</margin> *Kuti*, the first city of the Kingdom of *Nepal*. Although this mountainous tract is difficult to cross, nature has nevertheless provided a great abundance of waters breaking out everywhere from the caverns of the mountains, both hot and cold, as well as plenty of fish for men and pasture for the beasts. I think this tract is the same one which *Ptolemy* <note>The ancient Greek geographer</note> calls *Paropamisus*, a chain in the series of the *Caucasian* mountains, stretched far and wide toward the East and with its borders toward the South and North. *Marco Polo the Venetian* calls it *Belor*, and others by other names according to the diversity <margin>Belor.</margin> of the nations through which it passes.

<margin>Nesti, a city of the Kingdom of Nepal.</margin>
4. From *Kuti*, by a journey of five days, one arrives at the city of *Nesti*, in the Kingdom of *Nepal*, in which all live involved in the darkness of idolatry without any sign of the Christian faith; yet it abounds in all things necessary for sustaining life, so that 30 or 40 chickens are sold everywhere for one *scudo* <note>A silver coin</note>.

<margin>The Metropolis of Nepal, Kathmandu, 27 degrees.</margin>
5. From *Nesti*, one arrives in a journey of 6 days at the Metropolitan city of the Kingdom of *Nepal*, which is called *Kathmandu*, situated at 27 degrees and 5 minutes latitude, where a powerful King resides, who, although a pagan, is by no means hostile to the Christian law.

<margin>Bhadgaon.</margin>
6. From *Kathmandu*, by a journey of half a day, one arrives at the city of *Nepal*, the seat of the whole Kingdom, which they also call *Bhadgaon*.

<margin>The city of Hetauda, a colony of the Kingdom of Maranga, 26 degrees.</margin>
7. From *Nepal*, in a journey of five days, the city of *Hetauda* is encountered, a colony of the Kingdom of *Maranga*, situated at a latitude of 26 degrees and 36 minutes.

<margin>Mutgari, a city of the Mughals.</margin>
8. From *Hetauda*, one arrives in eight days at *Mutgari*, which is the first city of the *Mughal* Kingdom.

9. From *Mutgari*, a journey of ten days

# ATHANASIUS KIRCHER

<margin>The city of Patna in Bengal, 25 degrees.</margin>
...continues as far as Patna, which is a city of the Kingdom of Bengal on the Ganges, situated at a polar elevation <note>latitude</note> of 25 degrees and 44 minutes.

<margin>The city of Benares, 24 degrees.</margin>
10. From Patna, in the space of eight days, one arrives in Benares, a populous city on the Ganges, situated at a polar elevation of 24 degrees and 50 minutes; it is famous for the Academy of Brahmans <note>original: "Brachmanum Academiam"</note> which flourishes there, in which all the sciences native to the region—or more truly, unheard-of superstitions—are taught.

<margin>Katampur.</margin>
11. From Benares to Katampur is a journey of eleven days, and from there to Agra is seven days' journey.

<margin>Agra.</margin>
Thus, from Beijing <note>original: "Pekino"</note> all the way to Agra, the journey is continuous for 214 days; if you consider the delay of the Caravans, the journey is approximately one year and two months. And these things I received by word of mouth from the above-mentioned Fathers, who completed the journey just as we have described it.

---

## CHAPTER IV.

### Concerning the various dress, manners, and customs of the people of those Kingdoms, which the said Fathers, Albert d'Orville and Father Grueber, observed and depicted while passing through.

Just as the Kingdoms which the said Fathers traversed—on a journey hitherto attempted by no European—were unknown to Geographers, so too they observed many things hardly unworthy of consideration regarding the dress, manners, and customs of the nations. These they left to me, recorded partly in pictures and partly in writing, having taken great pains so that they might be inserted into the account of the journey they completed; which task I have now judged it opportune to perform.

<margin>Description of the Chinese Walls.</margin>
Setting out, therefore, from Beijing, the Metropolis of China <note>original: "Sinarum"</note> and the Imperial Seat, they arrived within the space of two months at the most famous walls. Situated at these is the huge city of Xining <note>original: "Siningfu"</note>, serving as a sort of very safe garrison of the walls against the Tartars. We have displayed the structure of these celebrated walls at the end of the Book, as diligently observed and sketched by them as the conditions of that place allowed. They added that the walls are of such width that six horsemen, arranged in a single row, can easily gallop along them without one hindering the other. Hence, they said the walls are frequently visited by the inhabitants of Xining, both to enjoy the very healthy air that blows from the adjacent sandy desert, and for the sake of performing other exercises to relax the mind, for which they are wonderfully suited. Indeed, the wall is so high that with a most open and pleasant prospect on every side, it easily entices the inhabitants to it, both for the reasons mentioned and because of the great convenience of the stairs that provide ascent to it. Furthermore, they say the length of this very wide wall, as far as the other gate through which one passes from the desert into the city of Suzhou <note>original: "Sucien"</note>, is so great that it can hardly be traversed in the space of eighteen days. Many people, led not so much by the necessity of conducting business as by curiosity, complete this trek, having first obtained permission from the Governor of Xining and being provided with sufficient provisions. For they say that from the wall, innumerable dwellings are seen as if looking down from a high mountain. Outside, however, in the adjacent desert—as they had heard told to them by word of mouth by the inhabitants—

<margin>Wild beasts of every kind, dwelling in the sandy desert at certain times of the year, are watched with impunity by the curious from the top of the wall.</margin>
wild beasts of every kind (such as Tigers, Lions, Elephants, Rhinoceroses, Leopards, Wild Bulls, and Monoceroses <note>original: "Monocerotes"; Kircher notes these are a species of horned asses, likely referring to the chiru or oryx</note>) provide wonderful and unusual spectacles as if from a high tower, where one is recreated while remaining immune from all dangers of the attacking beasts, especially...
